## 2026-11-07: Strata on this box, same model files, same token ids — RECORDED (the head-to-head the user asked for)

Status: `~/git/Strata/build-dsv41/strata-dsv41` (v0.2.10, engine 0.1.56, built here for sm_120; pack = Unsloth's
UD-IQ2_XXS shards 1-5 + ISTA's IQ4_NL Engram, because its Engram reader rejects Unsloth's Q5_K table; `--experts 6
--ssd-cache 95 --max-context 32768 --vision off --temperature 0`, `DS4_NO_EOS=1`, under `MemoryMax=110G`, one
run each, cold start). Ours from the harness and tonight's runs. Ids from our token files (`route1k`, `route16k`).

| measurement | Strata | QuixiInfer | ratio |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| decode, 256 tokens after a 64-token prompt, DSpark off | 27.5 tok/s | 29.2 (forced bench, warm, 1k) / ~23.8 (serve, concurrency 1, includes warm-up) | about even |
| decode, DSpark on (`--dspark-conf 0.65`) | 26.9 tok/s | — | DSpark gains nothing here (as analysed: bandwidth-bound) |
| prefill, 1k prompt | 148 tok/s | 91 (windows of 128, standard case); 63 in one cold 1024-row chunk | 1.6x Strata |
| prefill, 16k prompt, their chunk 1024 | 250 tok/s | 217 (chunks of 2048 + layer skip, cold) | 1.15x |
| prefill, 16k prompt, their chunk 4096 | 696 tok/s | 217 | 3.2x |
| prefill, 16k prompt, their chunk 8192 (their default class on one card) | **935 tok/s** | **217** | **4.3x** |

Caveats (both ways): Strata's prefill is not bit-identical to its decode (FP16 tensor-core tiles, cuBLAS FP16
dense), ours is; Strata read a 111 GB IQ4_NL Engram, we read the 135 GB Q5_K one; their runs are single and cold,
ours are the harness medians (prefill128) or single cold runs (16k chunks); their 16k figure excludes the load.
Reading: at decode the two engines are within a few percent either way, and DSpark does not help on this
hardware. At prefill the gap is structural and large: 8192-row chunks read once per layer with tensor-core GEMMs
(experts and dense), plus the prompt skip, against our 2048-row chunks of f32 per-token kernels: per 2048 rows
we spend 5.8 s of host launch overhead and 6.5 s of grouped-kernel time where Strata's whole 16k prompt takes
17.5 s. Closing a 4x with the current contract (prefill bit-identical to single steps, f32 chains) means a f32
GEMM-class dense path and an expert kernel at several times the current efficiency plus graphs or far fewer
launches: weeks. A tensor-core prefill path (what Strata does; our bf16 `qx_tmv` exists and was rejected for
bit-identity in 2026-11-04) would close most of it far sooner, at the price of prefill no longer matching
decode bit for bit (the reference check would move to a KLD tolerance for prompts).
Decision: STOP for the user's call on the contract (goal-prompt stop rule: a contract decision). Either way the
next engineering items are the same: fewer launches in the slab passes (looped multi-token GEMV, per-slab
graphs) and a cheaper expert kernel; the contract decides whether they are f32 or tensor-core kernels.
Tooling note: `QX_DS_BENCH=256` on a 64-token file faulted (status 700) in the committed binary; the shape is
unused by the harness; worth a look before it is relied on.

## 2026-11-07: One launch per dense GEMV group (token groups on blockIdx.z) — exact, NEUTRAL in time: the slab passes are GPU-bound

Status: `qx_mgemv_t8` with a token group per blockIdx.z (the same kernel instance per token, so the bits are the
eight-token launches'), one quantization launch for the window; `QX_MGEMV_ZGROUPS=0` restores the loop. Windows
whose token count is a multiple of 8 take it (the window path's graphs included). ds-checks: exact, including the
64-token window against single steps (128/128) and the 16k tails (chunks and windows, byte-identical).

| 16k prompt | before | z-groups | host enqueue of the slab passes → GPU remainder after it |
|---|--:|--:|---|
| chunks of 2048, KV-only | 4.8 ms/token | 5.0 | 2.7 s → 1.6 s enqueue, 0.3 s → 1.3 s sync (per 2048 rows) |
| chunks of 2048, full stack | 7.3 | 7.6 | 5.7 → 3.8 enqueue, 0.8 → 3.6 sync |
| windows of 128 with the skip | 8.9 | 9.2 | (graphs: launches were free already) |

Reading: the launches went from ~70k to ~20k per 2048 rows and the host enqueue fell accordingly, but the GPU
time that was hidden under the enqueue simply surfaced: enqueue + sync is 2.9 s (KV-only) / 7.4 s (full) either
way. The dense slab passes are GPU-bound at about 1.4 / 3.6 ms per row, the f32 per-token kernels' own cost at
128-row slabs, not a launch problem. Decision: kept (default on; it is exact, costs nothing and removes host time
that matters once the GPU side shrinks); the belief "the slab passes are launch-bound" is retracted. Next: the
nsys kernel table of a KV-only chunk (queued), then the kernels it names.

## 2026-11-07: Exact int8 tensor-core expert kernels (chunk path) — LANDED; the chunk's kernel profile — RECORDED

Status: `qx_moe_{gateup,down}_mma_<fmt>` (`mma.m16n8k32 s8`): lane (g, q) holds bytes 8q..8q+7 of a 32-element
sub-block for rows g and g+8 (the A operand) and for token g (the B operand), the permutation of k being the same
on both sides, so every 32-wide integer dot is exact; S = sum over sub-blocks of mult(row, sub) x dot is the
block's integer sum as `iq_terms` would sum it lane by lane, and the float chain per (row, selection) runs in
block order. Formats that scale per 16 elements (IQ2_S, IQ2_XS, Q2_K) take two mmas per sub-block with the A
fragment masked to each half (the first version used one and differed). Default in the chunk path
(`QX_DS_CHUNK_MMA=0`: the CUDA-core grouped kernels).

| 16k prompt, chunks of 2048 | grouped (CUDA cores) | int8 mma | tails |
|---|--:|--:|---|
| KV-only chunk: expert phase per chunk / ms per token | 3.75 s / 5.0 | **2.86 s / 4.6** (219 tok/s) | byte-identical |
| full stack: expert phase / ms per token | 6.6 s / 7.6 | **5.7 s / 7.2** | byte-identical |

The kernel table of two KV-only chunks and the kept tail (7,168 tokens, nsys, mma build; 28.8 s of kernels):

| kernel | share | calls | mean |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| `qx_mgemv_t8` (the dense GEMVs, 8-token groups) | **39.9%** | 21.7k | 529 us |
| `qx_ds_attn_part` + `merge` | 16.1% | 6.6k + 6.6k | 549 + 156 us |
| the mma gate/up kernels | 26.7% | 34k | 206-253 us |
| the mma down kernels | 8.5% | 34k | 65-75 us |
| `qx_moe_wait_word` | 4.7% | 34k | 39 us |
| everything else (hc, rms, rope, indexer, compress, combine) | under 4% | | |

Reading: the dense GEMV re-streams a group's weights for every 8 tokens (16 reads per 128-row slab) at about a
fifth of the memory peak: the biggest single item. A 16- or 32-token instance was tried and dropped: ptxas does not
finish in 7 minutes on it (the 16-format f32 path unrolled 16 ways; the same would hit NVRTC and the driver JIT at
every start). The exact fix is a tiled kernel that decodes a weight tile once into shared memory and applies it
to all 128 tokens with mgemv's per-token arithmetic unchanged. The expert kernels are now dequantization-bound
(an expert's 23.6M weights decoded per (expert, 64-token pass)), which is why chunk size pays twice: 8192-row
chunks decode and stream each expert a quarter as often per row. Attention at 128-row slabs re-reads the
window's keys per token.
Decision: LANDED (mma default). Next levers in order: chunks of 4096-8192 (the per-selection `gu`/`hq`/`hd`
become a per-pass scratch so the buffers fit), the tiled dense kernel, attention sharing the SWA keys across a
slab's tokens.

## 2026-11-07: Chunk size 2048 / 4096 / 8192 on the 16k prompt — 4096 is the card's sweet spot — RECORDED

Status: the mma build (0dad410), route16k, `QX_DS_RAM=80`, one run per arm; every arm's tail byte-identical.

| chunk rows | VRAM tier left (of 20 GiB asked) | 16k prompt, KV-only chunks (ms/token, tok/s) | 16k prompt, full stack | expert phase per row (full) | decode tail after the prompt |
|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| 2048 | 16.2 GiB | 4.6 / 219 | 7.2 / 139 | 2.8 ms | 51-55 ms |
| 4096 | 15.5 | **4.0 / 249** | 6.1 / 164 | 1.9 | 60-66 |
| 8192 | 13.9 | 4.0 / 249 | 5.3 / 187 | 1.0 | 60-62 |

Reading: the expert phase amortizes with the chunk as designed (each expert decoded and streamed once per chunk);
the dense slab passes cost the same per row at every size (about 3.5 ms full, 1.4 KV-only: the GPU work, split
differently between "enqueue" and "sync" as the chunk grows); the chunk buffers come off the VRAM expert tier
(0.9 / 1.8 / 3.5 GB), which the decode tail after the prompt pays. On a 16k prompt the KV-only part is one or two
chunks either way, so 4096 and 8192 tie there; 8192 wins only the full-stack tail.
Decision: 4096 for the harness's long-prompt cases (`prefill256k`, `long16k`: `QX_DS_CHUNK=4096` in their ARGS, a
case change to note on the next scoreboard row); the server defaults to 2048 for servers at `--ctx` of 32k or
more (the gentler VRAM cost; shorter servers keep windows) with `QX_DS_CHUNK` overriding. The buffers' VRAM
(`gu`/`out` per selection are the bulk) is the item to shrink before 8192 makes sense on a 32 GB card.

## 2026-11-07: The chunk path in the harness's long-prompt cases and in the server — LANDED: prefill256k 21.5 → 7.6 ms per token

Status: `prefill256k` and `long16k` run with `QX_DS_CHUNK=4096` (a case change: the chunk path is the engine's
prompt path now, in ds-check and in the server), the server takes prompt-only steps through `forward_chunk` with
2048-row chunks by default at `--ctx` 32k or more (`QX_DS_CHUNK` overrides; decoders never wait behind a chunk,
since a step with any decoder keeps the windows), and warms the chunk kernels before sealing its kernel set (the
first server run died on exactly that seal). Re-take of the three affected cases, 3 repeats, final tree:

| case | before (set D baseline) | now | spread |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| prefill256k (16k from 245,760; KV-only chunks + the kept tail) | 21.50 | **7.60** (-65%, 132 tok/s) | 2.6% |
| serve256k3 (short prompts; the 2048-row buffers off the tier) | 108.11 | 109.05 (+0.9%) | 1.7% |
| long16k: decode tail at 16k (companion) | 38.8 | 41.0 (+5.7%) | 4.4% |
| long16k-prefill (companion, ms per token of the 16k prompt) | 14.4 | **4.1** | |

Reading: the chunk path's gain lands in the scored case; the cost is the chunk buffers' VRAM (1.8 GB at 4096
rows, 0.9 at 2048), which the decode after a prompt pays through a smaller expert tier: +5.7% on long16k's tail
at 4096, +0.9% on serve256k3 at 2048. Set D projected from the medians: (52.5 x 7.6 x 109.05)^(1/3) = 35.2
against 49.6; the full `--check` run is queued for the row.
Decision: LANDED. Follow-ups: shrink the buffers (`gu`/`hq`/`hd` per selection become a per-pass scratch; `out`
could be accumulated per row in selection order), so 4096-8192 chunks cost the tier less; the server's chunk path
beside decoders (today a decoder present means windows).

## 2026-11-07: The dense GEMV's token groups inside one block (rows stay in L1) — LANDED pending ds-checks: -10 to -17% per chunk

Status: `qx_mgemv_t8` runs its token groups of 8 one after the other in the same block (the count in the high
bits of the token word), instead of a group per `blockIdx.z`: the same per-group code, so the same bits, and a
block's rows stay in L1 across the 16 groups of a 128-row slab (one HBM read of the weights per slab instead of
16). 16k prompt, chunks of 4096, one run each, tails byte-identical:

| | groups on blockIdx.z | groups in the block | |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| KV-only chunk (ms per row) | 2.48 | **2.24** | -10% |
| full-stack chunk (ms per row) | 5.56 | **4.53** | -17% (dense passes 11.0 + 3.5 s → 8.7 + 2.7 s) |
| 16k prompt, full stack (ms per token, tok/s) | 6.1 / 164 | **5.2 / 194** | |
| 16k prompt, KV-only chunks | 4.0 / 249 | 4.0 / 249 (the 544 s first-use JIT of the new source polluted this run's mean; the chunks say 2.24 against 2.48) | |

Operational note: a changed `mgemv.cu` costs about nine minutes of driver PTX JIT at the first launch on a box
(the source compiles pathologically slowly; ptxas offline takes ten minutes too); the cache (`~/.nv/ComputeCache`)
then holds it. The same hit lands on a server's first start after a build and would land on a user's first run:
shipping cubins for sm_120 instead of JIT-ing PTX is the fix (build.rs already runs nvcc).
Decision: LANDED once ds-checks on this build pass (queued); the window path takes the same code for widths that
are multiples of 8. The dense slab passes remain the largest item (about 2.8 ms per row full stack); the next step
there is the per-token kernels' share (attention 16%) and the GEMV's achieved bandwidth within L1.

## 2026-11-07: The server's chunked prompt path: time to first token on a 13.5k-token prompt 191 → 47 s — LANDED

Status: `quixiinfer serve --ctx 32768 --slots 2`, `QX_DS_RAM=95`, a 13,527-token prompt (46 KB of this repo's
docs), `max_tokens 1`, one request each on a fresh server (cold tier both times).

| prompt path | time to the first token | ms per prompt token |
|---|--:|--:|
| windows of 128 with the layer skip (the server before) | 191.0 s | 14.1 |
| chunks of 2048 (prompt-only steps; the layer skip inside) | **47.0 s** | **3.5** |

Same first token. Two seal fatals on the way, both from kernels the server had not warmed before sealing its
kernel set: the chunk path's kernels (now warmed in the server's warm-up) and `qx_mgemv_t7` from an 87-row
remainder slab (the chunk's remainder is now cut into the warmed widths: 64 + 16 + 6 + 1, never 5 or 7, as the
window scheduler and the replay do). Two more false starts were environmental: a lingering server holding 20 GB
of VRAM after its engine thread died, and a race with a queued ds-checks run.
Decision: LANDED (default 2048-row chunks on servers with 32k of context or more; `QX_DS_CHUNK` overrides). Open:
a decoder present keeps the windows (its tokens would wait seconds behind a chunk); a long prompt arriving while
others decode still gets the window path.

## 2026-11-07: decode256k's tier placement: the golden profile beats the saved ranking by 12-14% — RECORDED, the case decision pending its prefill256k pair

One run each, the 256k state resumed and 64 tokens decoded (chain-tail; `tail-*.log`), chunk11 binaries:

| warm-up ranking | QX_DS_PROMOS | ms per token (last 32) |
|---|--:|--:|
| saved beside the state (`wiki256k-262144.profile`) | 32 | 53.1 |
| saved | 128 | 52.3 |
| golden (`golden/profile-dsv41.txt`) | 32 | 46.6 |
| golden | 128 | 45.6 |

The saved ranking (the experts the 256k prompt used, written by qx-ds-check beside the state) is the wrong
warm-up for the decode tail: the decode steps' hot set is the model's general one, which is what the golden
profile ranks. The server already ranks its tiers by the golden profile and parked states carry no ranking, so
the bench case with the saved ranking is the unrepresentative one. Promotion rate (32 against 128 per token) is
worth 1-2%, within a single run's noise.

Decision rule (make the call by measurement): if prefill256k with the golden profile is not worse than with the
saved ranking (A/B queued in chain-ab3, same binary, 3 repeats each), both 256k resume cases drop the saved
ranking (a case-settings row on the scoreboard, like set B's tier change), and qx-ds-check keeps writing the
profile beside the state for the record only.

## 2026-11-07: The chunk path's per-pass gate/up and hidden scratch (indexed by list position) — LANDED: bit-identical, time-neutral, 0.4-0.8 GiB back to the tiers

The grouped and MMA expert kernels wrote gate/up and the quantized hidden rows per selection id (rows × k × ff
each), though one pass touches at most `rows` of them: the scratch is now indexed by a selection's place in its
expert's list (`lbase`), the down kernels read it there, and only the per-selection fallback
(QX_DS_CHUNK_GROUPED=0) keeps the k-times layout (the batched launch path, already rejected, needs that layout and
is off otherwise). route16k, chunk12 binaries, tail of 64 bit-identical against the window pass in every run:

| run | chunk11 (before) | chunk12 (per-pass scratch) |
|---|--:|--:|
| 4096, layer skip: VRAM budget left to the tiers | 15.5 GiB | 15.9 GiB |
| 4096, layer skip: the KV-only chunk | 9268 ms | 9274 ms |
| 4096, full stack: the chunk | 18557 ms (193.5 tok/s) | 18707 ms (191.8 tok/s) |
| 8192, layer skip: VRAM budget | 13.9 GiB (pre-GEMV-groups binary) | 14.7 GiB |
| 8192, layer skip: the 16k prompt | 249.3 tok/s (pre-GEMV-groups binary) | 283.4 tok/s |

Time-neutral at 4096 (within 1%), as expected: the scratch is written and read once either way. The point is the
VRAM at large chunks: at 8192 rows the gate/up scratch alone was 8192 × 8 × 2 × ff f32. Note the 8192 row: with
the GEMV groups and the smaller scratch, 8192-row chunks now run the 16k prompt at 283 tok/s against 227 for 4096
in the same binary (sc-c4096-skip), which reopens the chunk-size question for prefill256k (4096 since 6d8ce02)
and the server (2048): queued (chain-ab4), the VRAM at 256k context being the constraint.

## 2026-11-07: A q8-only 16-token GEMV instance (`qx_mgemv_q8_t16`) for the chunk's planar Q8_0 segments — REJECTED: exact, +4 to +7% per chunk

The idea: with in-block token groups, a wider instance (16 tokens per weight read) halves the split-K block
latencies per row; the 16- and 32-token instances of the full template do not finish in ptxas, but a q8-only
one (only `row_partial_q8` instantiated, 176 registers, no spills) does. The planar Q8_0 segments took it (mode
0, int8 inputs), the other segments the 8-token instance, knob QX_MGEMV_Q16=0 for the old split. ds-checks pass
(exact 6/6, production 57/59 KLD 0.128643, windows 128/128, rewind 64/64); route16k tails bit-identical. Chunk
times, same tree otherwise (chunk12 against chunk13), the steady chunks:

| run | chunk12 (8-token groups) | chunk13 (q8 segments on 16-token groups) |
|---|--:|--:|
| 4096 full stack, chunks 2-4 | 18707 / 18546 / 18362 ms | 19402 / 19403 / 19186 ms (+3.7 to +4.6%) |
| 4096 layer skip, the KV-only chunks | 9274 / 9270 ms | 9934 / 9904 ms (+7%) |
| 4096 layer skip, the last (full) chunk | 18782 ms | 19375 ms (+3.2%) |

(The first chunk of each run carries the kernel warm-up and is not compared.) Slower in every chunk: at 16
tokens the per-thread accumulators and the x fragments double, and the loss of occupancy on the split-K rows
outweighs the halved block count; the 8-token group with rows in L1 is already memory-efficient. Reverted (the
kernel instance would also cost JIT time at first use); the record is this entry and the chunk13 binary in
~/qx-bins.

## 2026-11-07: The in-block GEMV groups cost the decode step 4.7% through the shared template — RECORDED; the split (`_t8z` entries) is in the tree, verification queued

The set D row's decode-side drift (+1 to +4.5% on every decode case, serve256k3 flagged) traced to the one
decode-side change in that tree. The screening pair, two rounds, binaries swapped between runs (4 GiB JIT cache;
`ab3-*.log`):

| binary | decode (ms/token, spread) | prefill128 (windows of 128) |
|---|--:|--:|
| chunk9: `mgemv.cu` before the groups | 32.65 (0.3%, 2 of 3 repeats: the first timed out in the driver JIT), 32.80 | 11.70, 11.70 |
| chunk11: the promoted tree (in-block groups) | 34.20 (2.3%), 34.20 | 10.90, 10.90 |
| chunk11 with QX_MGEMV_ZGROUPS=0 | 34.30 | 11.30 |

Decode +4.7% with the groups' template, and the knob does not recover it: widths 1-8 never take the one-launch
path, so the cost is the template itself (the runtime group loop, the hoisted `part` array and the trailing
`__syncthreads` in every instance, width 1 included). prefill128 is the other way: -6.8% with the one-launch
path (one launch per projection for 16 groups, rows in L1), of which about half (11.30 → 10.90) is the one-launch
path and the rest the template as well.

Fix in the tree: `mgemv_group` is the pre-groups one-group code; the width 1-8 entries call it once (their code is
again what chunk9 compiled), and three new entries (`qx_mgemv_t8z`, `_silu_t8z`, `_silumul_t8z`) carry the group
loop for the one-launch path alone (`ZENTRIES` in `ops.rs`). Expected: decode back to 32.7, prefill128 at or
near 10.9, the chunk's slab passes unchanged (they use the z entries). Queued as chain-ab5: nvcc with register
counts, build (chunk14), ds-checks, the screening pair against chunk11 twice, the 16k chunk timing against
chunk12's 18707 ms.

| binary | decode | prefill128 | 16k chunk (rows 4096-16320, full stack) |
|---|--:|--:|--:|

Registers across the versions of `mgemv.cu` (ptxas `--resource-usage`, `mgemv-vers/regs-*.log`):

| version | `qx_mgemv` (width 1) | `_t3` | `_t8` | `_silu` | `_silumul_t8` | `_t8z` |

So the promoted tree's decode cost was the width 1-3 entries going from 80 to 128 registers (the runtime group

## 2026-11-07: 256k decode kernel profile on 0ced38e (golden profile) — RECORDED: 57% of the step is the GPU waiting for experts; the indexer is now 9%

nsys of the resumed 262,144-token state, 64 greedy tokens (`nsys-dec256k2-kern_cuda_gpu_kern_sum.csv`; 50.6 ms per
token under the profiler, 46.4 in the harness). GPU time per step 49.4 ms:

| kernel | per step | share | note |
|---|--:|--:|---|
| `qx_moe_wait_staged` | 17.0 ms (38.8 calls × 439 us) | 34.5% | the GPU spinning for experts DMA'd from pinned RAM |
| `qx_moe_wait` | 10.8 ms (38.8 × 277 us) | 21.8% | spinning for the CPU pool's expert results |
| `qx_mgemv` (width 1) | 6.5 ms (511 × 12.7 us) | 13.2% | the dense projections |
| `qx_ds_topk` | 2.5 ms (8.7 × 284 us) | 5.0% | one block, four radix passes over 262k scores |
| `qx_ds_idx_score2` | 2.2 ms (7.8 × 280 us) | 4.4% | was 8.8 ms × 8 = 70 before idx_score2 |
| `qx_ds_attn_part` + `merge` | 1.9 ms | 3.9% | |
| `qx_rms_groups` | 1.2 ms (234 × 5 us) | 2.4% | |
| expert kernels (q8k gateup/down, all formats) | ~4.2 ms | ~8.5% | |
| everything else (hc, rope, route, combine, q8x) | ~3 ms | ~6% | |

Tier shares over the 64 tokens: vram 7245, staged 2894, ram 4820, nvme 441 selections (per token about 113 from
VRAM, 45 DMA'd, 75 computed by the CPU pool, 7 from NVMe). So a 256k decode step is 28 ms of waiting for experts
that are not in VRAM and 21 ms of kernels; the position-proportional part (indexer + top-k + attention, 6.6 ms)
is now a seventh of the step. The waits are per layer and on the critical path: routing at the end of a layer's
attention, then the host fetch, then the MoE. At 1k context the same waits make most of the 33 ms step.

Levers that follow (family 9 re-aimed, family "expert latency" new): (a) hide the per-layer fetch behind the
previous layers' compute with a speculative prefetch (the previous token's experts for the same layer, or a
routing guess from the pre-attention residual), exactness untouched because the selection stays the routed one;
(b) the staging path's latency itself (439 us for a handful of ~1 MB rows: DMA issue, ring, signal; 25 GB/s says
0.25 ms of transfer); (c) the CPU pool's per-layer latency (277 us); (d) `qx_ds_topk` as a multi-block select
(2.5 ms, 5%); (e) `qx_mgemv` at width 1 (6.5 ms: 511 launches of 12.7 us, launch-bound; fuse the small
projections per layer, backlog family 8).

## 2026-11-07: The speculative-prefetch premise measured — RECORDED, the naive form killed: the previous token predicts 13-23% of the host-served experts

Counters added to the driver (`spec_hits`/`spec_total`: a layer's routed experts also routed by the previous
token; `spec_miss_*`: the same among the selections the host serves, pool + staged; printed on the verbose
stats line). One run each (`spec-1k.log`, `spec-256k.log`):

| run | all selections repeating | host-served selections repeating |
|---|--:|--:|
| route1k forced decode, 1024 tokens | 91,874 / 245,794 = 37% | 9,891 / 73,159 = 13.5% |
| 256k resumed decode, 64 tokens | 5,666 / 15,400 = 37% | 1,851 / 8,155 = 22.7% |

The experts that repeat are the ones promotion has already put in VRAM; the host-served tail has little
temporal locality. A prefetch guessed from the previous token could hide at most 13-23% of the 28 ms of waits
(5 ms per step at 256k at best, before the cost of 77-86% wasted copies), under the 8% bar for its complexity
(a double-buffered staging area, prefetch bookkeeping in `serve`). Killed in this form; a better predictor would
need the next layer's routing input, which does not exist before the layer runs.

What the counters also say: `pool_ns` is 29.5 ms per step at 256k (0.76 ms per layer) against `stage_ns` 2.7 ms:
the staged copies are done when the pool finishes; the per-layer latency is the CPU pool computing about two
expert-token products (~13 MB of IQ2 weights at an effective 17 GB/s, a third of the RAM's bandwidth). Next:
the split between staged (DMA to the GPU's expert kernels) and pool-computed RAM experts, `QX_DS_STAGE_SHARE`,
on decode256k and decode.

## 2026-11-07: The decode staging share (`QX_DS_STAGE_SHARE_DECODE`, of 8) — RECORDED: the default 3 is the optimum at 256k and at 1k; DMA and the pool cost the same per record

chunk18 binary (target/release), 3 repeats per arm (`stg-*.log`). The share is the fraction of a layer's RAM-resident
experts DMA'd into the staging area for the GPU's expert kernels; the rest the CPU pool computes.

| share | decode256k (ms/token) | tier shares per 64 tokens (vram / staged / pool / nvme) | decode at 1k |
|---|--:|---|--:|
| 0 (all pool) | 47.40 (1.1%) | 9445 / 0 / 5403 / 552 | 34.30 (0.9%) |
| 3 (default) | **45.40** (1.1%) | 9443 / 2026 / 3381 / 550 | 33.00 (2.7%) |
| 5 | 46.00 (1.1%) | 9447 / 3375 / 2026 / 552 | — |
| 8 (all DMA) | 46.10 (1.1%) | 9444 / 5402 / 2 / 552 | 32.70 (0.3%) |

All-DMA and all-pool land within 2-4% of each other at both contexts: a record (~9.5 MB) costs about the same
whether the copy engines move it to the GPU or sixteen cores read it, because both read the same memory bus
and the per-layer round trip (issue, transfer, done-word) is the latency. Splitting across the two paths wins a
little (3/8), as the 2026-11-06 measurement said. Nothing to change; the knob stays. The lever on the 28 ms of
waits is therefore fewer host-served records per token (VRAM residency) or pipelining across layers (which
needs a routing prediction the previous token does not provide: 23%).

## 2026-11-07: `qx_ds_topk2`, the tiled one-block top-k — LANDED: decode256k 45.4 → 43.5-44.2 (-3 to -4%), the same selection bit for bit

The original kernel (one block per token, 4 radix passes over a shared 256-bin histogram with 1,024 threads on the
atomics, then a selection loop with two barriers per 1,024 rows) was 284 us per call at 262k rows, 8.7 calls per
step, 2.5 ms of a 46 ms step. `qx_ds_topk2` keeps the algorithm and the output order (every key above the k-th in
ascending index order, then the first `need` equal keys in ascending order: the reference's tie rule) with
per-warp histograms reduced once per pass and a selection pass over tiles of 1,024 x 8 rows (one block scan per
tile, each thread 8 consecutive rows, one sector). `QX_DS_TOPK_KERNEL=1` keeps the old one.

Exactness: ds-checks (exact 6/6, production 57/59 KLD 0.128643, windows 128/128, rewind 64/64); the route16k
chunk tail bit-identical (the top-k at n up to 16k rows); the 64 greedy tokens of the resumed 256k decode
identical between the kernels. Harness, chunk19, same session (`topk-*.log`):

| kernel | decode256k (ms/token) |
|---|--:|
| `qx_ds_topk` (old) | 45.40 (2.0%) |
| `qx_ds_topk2` | 43.50 (0.9%), 44.20 (3.4%) |

A 3-4% win on a scored case, under the 8% kernel bar; kept because the result is verified identical three ways,
the knob reverts it, and it removes a serial kernel from the critical path (the candidate-block top-k runs on it

## 2026-11-07: What a non-exact (FP16 tile) prefill costs — MEASURED on Strata against our exact path and the f32 reference: nothing measurable beyond the engine's own numerics

The user's question: characterize the degradation Strata takes for its 4.5x prefill, and whether exactness is worth
keeping. Teacher-forced next-token distributions on the same token ids (`tools/logits_compare.py`; Strata's
`--score`, `DS4_SCORE_PREFIX` feeds a prefix through its long-prompt path, `--window` > 16 sends the scored windows
through the tile path too; ours: logits dumps of the window pass, exact = decode bits). KL is KL(reference || run)
over the reference's 64 most likely tokens for Strata rows (its own `compare` rule), full-vocabulary between dumps.

**16k text, positions 12,288-16,383 (4,095 scored), reference = our exact production path (perplexity 2.493):**

| Strata arm | top-1 agreement | mean KL | p99 KL | perplexity |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| prefix 12,288 through the FP16 tiles, scored in decode windows of 16 | 94.5% | 0.0288 | 0.280 | 2.539 (+1.8%) |
| prefix through the tiles, scored through the tiles (windows of 128) | 94.3% | 0.0275 | 0.293 | 2.531 (+1.5%) |
| everything through decode windows of 16 (no tiles) | 94.4% | 0.0263 | 0.292 | 2.541 (+1.9%) |

The three arms are indistinguishable: an FP16-tile prefix, or scoring through the tiles, moves nothing against
Strata's own no-tile path. The distance to our exact path (KL ~0.027, perplexity +1.5-1.9%) is the engine's
general numerics (FP16 K/V and activations), present in every arm.

**1k text against the f32 reference (`QX_MGEMV_INT8=0 QX_EXACT_EXPERTS=1`, 418 ms per token, perplexity 2.515
over 1,023 positions; 2.989 over positions 512+):**

| run | positions | top-1 vs f32 | mean KL | p99 | perplexity |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| ours, production (int8 GEMV inputs, int8 expert kernels) | 1,023 | 95.9% | 0.0197 | 0.227 | 2.517 (+0.1%) |
| Strata, decode windows | 1,023 | 94.0% | 0.0457 | 0.594 | 2.561 (+1.8%) |
| ours, production | 512+ (511) | 96.1% | 0.0107 | 0.132 | 3.027 (+1.3%) |
| Strata, prefix 512 through the tiles, scored through the tiles | 511 | 93.2% | 0.0251 | 0.293 | 2.949 (-1.3%) |
| Strata, decode windows | 511 | 94.3% | 0.0259 | 0.306 | 2.997 (+0.3%) |

Our production path sits at KL 0.01-0.02 from f32 (the ds-checks figure of 0.13 is 59 positions of one prompt);
Strata's at 0.025-0.046, about twice as far, in every path alike; the perplexity differences (±1-2%) are inside
what 511-1,023 positions resolve. Conclusion: a tensor-core prefill with bf16/FP16 matmul inputs costs at most
Strata's engine-wide KL of ~0.03 against an exact path, and less if the accumulation, the K/V store and the
expert path stay as they are (the int8 expert kernels already run on tensor cores exactly). What it costs us is
the contract itself: prompt bits equal to decode bits, which the windows/chunks identity checks and prefix reuse
rest on; those checks would become KL-bounded. Decision: not taken here; the numbers go to the user.

## 2026-11-07: Chunk-path kernel profile on the landed tree (chunk19) — RECORDED: the dense one-launch GEMV is 42% and L2-bound on re-reading the activation slab per row

nsys over two 4096-row chunks of route16k (8,192 tokens, full stack; `prof-chunk19-kern_cuda_gpu_kern_sum.csv`,
`prof-chunk19-trace_cuda_gpu_trace.csv`). GPU time 33.7 s; per slab-layer (64 slabs x 40 layers):

| kernel | share | launches per slab-layer | avg |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| `qx_mgemv_t8z` (dense projections, one launch per group) | 41.9% | 13.4 | 413 us |
| `qx_ds_attn_part` + `merge` | 22.1% | 4.1 + 4.1 | 560 + 157 us |
| expert gate/up mma (iq2_xxs, iq2_s, iq1_s) | 24.2% | ~10 | 290-320 us |
| expert down mma | ~7% | ~10 | 83-100 us |
| the rest (hc mixes, quant_h, idx_score2, rms, q8x) | ~5% | | |

The GEMV launches by grid (= blocks = rows for the split-K segments): 5,120 rows 817 us (2 per slab-layer, 31% of
the GEMV time), 1,024 rows 119 us (8 per slab-layer, the output-A head groups, 24%), 4,992 rows 673 us (16%),
4,096 rows 741 us (14%), 8,192 rows 1,167 us (Engram layers, 5%). A 5,120-row planar-Q8 launch reads 21 MB of
weights (14 us at HBM rate) but, one row per block, streams the slab's int8 activations (512 KB) and scales
through L2 once per row: 2.6 GB, 520 us at L2 rate. That is the kernel: L2-bound on the operand that should be
the small one. Blocking RB rows per block divides the x traffic by RB with every (row, token) chain kept in the
reference order (same (warp, lane) -> segment mapping, a0/a1 alternation, `(d * xd) * dot`, warp_sum, the 8-warp
sum). Written as `mgemv_zr<XM, NT, RB>` (`qx_mgemv_*_t8z4` / `_t8z8`, knob `QX_MGEMV_ZR`, tables with ceil(rows /
RB) blocks for the Q8 split-K segments); the f32-path segments (Q6_K output-A, 24%) keep the one-row code for
now. Expected: the Q8 launches 3-4x faster, the dense passes about -50%, the chunk about -25%.

## 2026-11-07: Rows per block on the one-launch GEMV (`mgemv_zr`, 4 and 8 rows sharing the activation fragments) — REJECTED: bit-identical, 12% slower at either count

Same binary (chunk20), route16k full stack at 4096, alternating (`zr-*.log`), tails bit-identical in every run,
ds-checks pass at 4 rows per block:

| rows per block | chunks 2-4 (ms) |
|---|--:|
| 1 (`qx_mgemv_t8z`) | 18851 / 18563 / 18420 |
| 4 | 21215 / 20956 / 20921 and 21836 / 21033 / 20835 |
| 8 | 21020 / 20863 / 20755 |

The same cost at 4 and at 8 rows refutes the L2 hypothesis: if the activation re-reads were the time, 4 rows
would have cut them 4x and 8 rows 8x. What fits is latency per group: each block walks its 16 token groups
serially, each with a dependent chain (load, dp4a, fma), a warp_sum, two barriers and a shared round trip, about
3 us a group, and the row blocking lengthens that serial work per lane while cutting the blocks that could overlap
it. Code removed (six kernel instances were first-use JIT cost). Next form, written as `mgemv_zb` (`QX_MGEMV_ZB=1`):
one barrier per block, the warps' partials for every (group, token) written to shared memory as computed and
summed once at the end in the same warp order.

## 2026-11-07: The dense GEMV in a micro-benchmark (`kernels/bench/mgemv_bench.cu`) — what the one-launch kernel is bound by, and a 2.4x form

Two engine-level forms in a row refuted their hypotheses on 20-minute runs (rows per block with per-group barriers
+12%, one barrier per block +3%, both bit-identical), so the kernel now has a standalone bench: one planar-Q8_0
segment (rows x cols), a 128-token int8 slab with its scales, the engine's table and info words, every variant
timed over 20 launches and its output compared bit for bit with `qx_mgemv_t8z`. Build in a minute, run in seconds
(`~/qx-bins/mgemv_bench [rows] [cols] [tokens]`). 5,120 x 4,096 x 128 (the g_ffn launch's shape):

| form | us | TMAC/s | bits |
|---|--:|--:|---|
| `t8z` (current) | 516 | 5.2 | reference |
| `t8zb` (one barrier per block) | 512 | 5.2 | identical |
| `t8zt` (one barrier, 4 rows per block sharing the activation fragments) | **217** | 12.4 | identical |
| `t8zt` with 8 rows | 279 | 9.6 | identical (spills) |
| row per lane, the lane positions walked in the thread, the tree folded with a stack | 533 | 5.0 | identical |
| 4 rows, the trees folded after a shared-memory transpose (no shuffles) | 289 | 9.3 | identical |
| `t8zt` diagnostic: no reduction trees | 153 | | not exact |
| `t8zt` diagnostic: no dot products (loads only) | 215 | | not exact |
| `t8zt` with base + stride addressing / forced 2 blocks per SM | 217 / 500 | | identical |

At 1,024 rows (the output-A group launches): 117 -> 56 us. What the numbers say: the barriers were never the time
(engine: the one-barrier form +3%; bench: -1%); the activation re-reads are (4 rows sharing them: 2.4x), but not
further (8 rows spill); the remaining 217 us is load instructions (removing the dots changes nothing: ~0.5 loads
per 8-element step, the practical floor at this register budget) plus the warp_sum trees (30%); folding the tree
with a stack is exact (verified against the shuffle tree bit for bit, in emulation and on the card) but serial, so
slower than the butterfly's parallel levels. The rows-per-block form with per-group barriers was 12% slower in
the engine, which the bench does not reproduce; the engine test of `t8zt` (chunk22, QX_MGEMV_ZT=1) is running.
Expected in the engine: the Q8 launches (76% of the GEMV time) 2.4x, the dense passes about -45%, the chunk
about -18%. The f32-path segments (Q6_K output-A, 24%) want the same row sharing next.

## 2026-11-07: Bench, second round — the one-launch GEMV is latency-bound, not traffic-bound; the f32 path gains from decoding once per block

More forms in `mgemv_bench` (5,120 x 4,096 x 128 planar Q8 unless noted; all bit-identical):

| form | us | note |
|---|--:|---|
| `t8zt` (4 rows sharing the fragments, persistent a0/a1, one barrier) | 212 | the base |
| weights held across the 16 groups (`zh`), Q8 | 216 | no gain: the x loads are the loads |
| transient accumulators, rows walked serially: 8 / 16 / 32 rows | 254 / 239 / 432 | more rows per block does not pay |
| transient v, batched like `zt`: 4 / 6 / 8 rows (149 / 162 / 243 registers) | 410 / 387 / 327 | slower than `zt` at the same 4 rows |
| planar Q6_K (f32 path), 1,024 rows: current / 2 rows shared / 2 rows + decode once per block (`zh`) | 240 / 243 / **176** | the f32 path's gain |

What refutes the traffic story: 16 and 32 rows per block cut the activation re-reads 4-8x and are slower. What is
left is latency hiding: the 4-rows form keeps 32 (row, token) chains and their 32 trees in flight per warp at 1-2
blocks per SM, and every form that computes fewer pairs between dependent phases, or spends more registers, loses.
The bench's 2.4x on the Q8 launches and 1.36x on the f32 ones stand; the engine kernel (`qx_mgemv_*_t8zt`) now
carries both: 4 rows per block on the planar Q8 split-K segments, 2 rows with the decode once per block on the
others at cols <= 4096 (the table gives those blocks accordingly). Two engine tests queued: chunk22 (the Q8 branch
alone, running) and chunk24 (both branches, after the slab attention's test).

## 2026-11-07: Slab attention (`qx_ds_attn_tile`) in the engine — bit-identical, 5% slower; and the chunk path's real bottleneck: one metadata upload per launch

`QX_DS_ATTN=tile` on chunk23, ds-checks pass (windows 8 and 64 bit-identical: the in-block merge reproduces the two
kernels' bits), the route16k tail bit-identical. Alternating with the chunked kernels, the CPU quiet for the second
pair (`attn-*.log`): tile 19634 / 19503 / 19302 and 19764 / 19502 / 19228 ms against part 18675 / 18432 / 18332 and
19127 / 18678 / 19542. Slower by about 5%: 256 blocks per call each walking 54 key chunks twice in series, against
13,800 small blocks; reuse bought, parallelism lost. The whole-window slice (1,024 blocks per call; the partials
buffer is scratch in this mode) is in the tree for the next test.

The finding that matters more, from the chunk19 trace: per slab-layer the dense passes are 55 kernel launches and
**93 host-to-device copies of 28 us each** (CubeCL stages every launch's info words into a device buffer; its cache
of identical info words misses in the chunk path because the slab views change the pointers every slab), 2.6 ms of
the 11.5 ms of GPU time per slab-layer on the compute stream, and on the host a blocking small copy per launch: the
chunk log's "slab enqueue 8.7 s" of an 18.6 s chunk, against "sync on the slab passes 2.7 s". The chunk is
launch-bound, which is why a 2.4x faster GEMV (bench) moved the chunk by nothing in the engine, and why the GEMV
and attention forms both read as +5% there. The lever is the launch path: info words that do not travel per launch
(one staging copy per slab pass, or a pinned ring with async copies, or by-value kernel parameters), and fewer,
fused launches per slab-layer.

## 2026-11-07: The 4-rows GEMV inside the engine (nsys per launch) and the host's launch cost (perf) — RECORDED

**Per launch, chunk24 with QX_MGEMV_ZT=1 against chunk19** (`prof-zt2-trace`, `prof-chunk19-trace`; medians):
the planar-Q8 launches halve as the bench said (5,120 rows: 817 -> 409 us at 1,280 blocks; 1,024 rows: 119 -> 58;
the mixed g_ffn 673 -> 445), but the same kernel's one-row fallback, which the Q6_K / Q5_K layers' segments take
(their output-B at 5,120 x 8,192: about 1,774 -> 3,289 us), runs 1.85x slower than `qx_mgemv_t8z` did: a kernel's
registers are the union of its branches, and the Q8 branch's 150 took the one-row code's occupancy from two blocks
per SM to one. Total GEMV time per slab-layer: 5.52 -> 5.54 ms. The fix in the tree: one kernel per segment kind on
its own sub-table (`_t8zt` for planar-Q8 split-K at 4 rows, `_t8zf` for the other split-K segments at <= 4,096
columns with 2 rows and the decode once per block, `_t8z` for the rest), up to three launches per group.

**The host, `perf record` on the engine during a chunk** (`perf-chunk.txt`): 25% of the samples in CubeCL's
`ChannelDeviceHandle::submit_inner` (the client-to-server submission per launch), 16% in its memory pool
(`MemoryPage::coalesce` 12%, `try_reserve` 3%: the per-launch info buffer's allocation and free), 12% the driver
thread's loop, the rest libcuda. So the ~120 us a launch costs on the host is CubeCL's submission and pool work, not
the driver; the info-cache hit path (the patched cubecl-cuda, `QX_INFO_CACHE_ENTRIES`) skips the pool work and the
copy; the submission itself only goes away with fewer launches or a launcher that bypasses the channel.

## 2026-11-07: A 262,144-entry CubeCL info cache — REJECTED: the steady chunk 18.6 → 46.6 s; the raw launcher is the fix

The patched cubecl-cuda (`QX_INFO_CACHE_ENTRIES`, chunk25). The big cache does make the slab passes' info words hit
from the second chunk on, but CubeCL's memory pool does not scale to the ~140k small live buffers it then holds:
the second 4,096-row chunk took 46,557 ms against 18,609 with the default 4,096 entries (`cache-*.log`; the perf
profile already showed `MemoryPage::coalesce` at 12% of the host at 4,096 entries). The first chunk of that run
took 38 minutes: the engine's NVRTC validation of the changed `mgemv.cu` with a real target (a full ptxas pass,
now PTX-only: 896fd4b) and then the driver JIT. The control arm: 18,854 / 18,609 / 18,682 ms, slab enqueue 8.8 s,
sync 2.7 s, expert enqueue 6.7 s. The patch stays (behaviour-neutral without the variable) for the stream handle it
now also exposes.

Decision: bypass CubeCL's launch path for the chunk pass. `qx_gpu::raw` (in the tree, `QX_RAW_LAUNCH=1`, scoped to
`forward_chunk_opt`): the same flattened sources compiled with NVRTC to PTX once per module and loaded with
`cuModuleLoadData`, every launch a memcpy into a pinned ring slot, one `cuMemcpyHtoDAsync` of the info words and one
`cuLaunchKernel` on CubeCL's own stream (`cubecl::cuda::QX_STREAMS`, the vendored crate's one addition), so the
launches stay ordered with CubeCL's uploads and syncs; never during a graph capture. Expected: the host's ~120 us a
launch to a few, the 28 us copies on the compute stream to ~2, the slab enqueue 8.7 s and the expert enqueue 6.9 s
of an 18.6 s chunk mostly gone; the chunk then bounded by its 11.5 s of GPU time, where the 4-rows GEMV and the
other kernel forms finally show. Test queued (chain-raw) behind the per-kind GEMV kernels' chain.

## 2026-11-08: Per-record PCIe landing time — MEASURED (0.19 ms for 9.5 MB, at the bus ceiling; the 0.7 ms staged copy is driver-side)

`kernels/bench/pcie_bench.cu` (no model; nvcc, seconds): a 9.5 MB record from pinned memory lands in 0.194 ms by
`cudaMemcpyAsync` on one stream (49.0 GB/s, PCIe 5.0 x16's practical ceiling), 0.222 ms per record with 24 in flight
on one stream (42.9 GB/s), 0.227 with 24 over 4 streams (41.8: the engines serialize anyway), and a gather kernel
reading GPU-mapped pinned memory with int4 loads lands one in 0.196 ms at 48, 170 or 680 blocks (48.4 GB/s) and 24
in 0.199 ms each (47.8 GB/s). So the gather-from-mapped form Big-MoE-Small-Box uses buys nothing on this bus: copy
engine and SMs both saturate it. The 0.7 ms per staged record measured in the engine (2026-11-06) is therefore not
the transfer: it is what sits around it in the driver (the staging slot's descriptor write, the host-side wait for the
slot, the MB_SEQ protocol). Backlog family 15 idea 2 becomes "cut the per-record overhead in the driver to the
0.2 ms the bus takes", worth up to 93 × 0.5 = ~45 ms per 256k decode token in principle, less in practice because the
copies overlap compute. Needs an nsys trace of a 256k decode step to split the 0.7 ms (where the GPU idles between
records vs. where the host does).

## 2026-11-08: Staged records' readiness by per-stream words instead of the driver's done word — INCONCLUSIVE (equal), kept as `QX_DS_STG_WAIT=words`

The driver posted the staged-done word after waiting for the copies *and* after its pool share of the same job, so the
GPU's staged expert kernels ran only once the CPU pool was done. `qx_moe_wait_staged2` polls one device word per stage
stream that the driver writes behind that stream's copies (the job's sequence number), and the driver no longer waits
for the copies at all. Exact by construction; ds-checks pass with it. Set D A/B/B/A on chunk29 (words / done):
decode256k 44.70, 44.60 vs 44.80, 44.90; decode 34.40, 34.40 vs 34.30, 34.50. Nothing: the pool share finishes no
sooner than the copies, the staged kernels after it take tens of microseconds, and the GPU ends up waiting on the pool
word either way. The driver's counters say where the 28 ms per 256k token goes: the pool window is 22 ms per token
(about 47 records through the pool at 0.37 ms each on 16 cores, including 4.8 ms of NVMe fetch waits on 7 records per
token); the staged copies are not on the critical path. Default stays the done word; the words path is the knob to
pair with a higher decode staging share (tested next).

## 2026-11-08: Decode staging share 8/8 and 5/8 under the ready-words path — INCONCLUSIVE (flat at 256k), 3/8 stays

With the staged copies no longer gated by the pool word (`QX_DS_STG_WAIT=words`), a higher decode staging share
might have moved pool work to the bus. chunk29, decode256k / decode, arms 8, 5, 3, 8, 5 (eighths):
8/8 45.00 / 33.60 and 45.40 / 33.80; 5/8 45.90 / 35.00 and 45.90 / 34.00; 3/8 (control) 45.70 / 35.00. decode256k
is flat within 2% whatever the share (the pool window is set by the few records per layer and their latency, not
by how many the bus takes); decode at 1k is 2% better at 8/8 than the same-session controls (34.0-35.0), inside
the bar. A niced ptxas compile ran on the SMT siblings during these arms. No default change; the share and the
words path remain knobs.

## 2026-11-08: The CPU pool on all 32 hardware threads (`QX_DS_CPUS=0-31`) — REJECTED (decode256k 67.8, decode 51.4)

One thread per SMT sibling as well as per core (16 cores, 32 threads on the 9950X), under the words path: decode256k
67.80 ms per token (spread 194%: the steps stall in bursts), decode 51.40, against 45-46 / 34-35 with the 16-core
pool. The siblings share the cores' execution units and caches with the pool's own threads, and the driver and
engine threads are left nothing to run on while 32 workers spin at the barriers. The remaining arms were skipped.
The pool stays on the 16 physical cores; the lever on its per-record time is the barrier structure and the idle
cores' clocks (backlog family 15, items 9-10), not more threads.

## 2026-11-08: Software E4M3 conversions for cards before sm_89 — VERIFIED bit for bit against the instructions

`kernels/bench/e4m3_check.cu` on the 5090: every one of the 2^32 float patterns through `cvt.rn.satfinite.e4m3x2.f32`
against `f32_to_e4m3_sw`, every byte pair through `cvt.rn.f16x2.e4m3x2` + f16->f32 against `e4m3_to_f32_sw`. First
run: 16,777,214 mismatches (exactly the NaN inputs: the instruction gives 0x7f whatever the sign) and 511 (exactly
the pairs with a NaN byte: through f16 the result is the positive all-ones NaN 0x7fffffff). With those two rules
0 / 0. So an RTX 30-series card's FP8 KV cache holds the same bytes a 40/50-series card's does, and reads them back
to the same floats. (The DeepSeek path has no FP8; this is the Qwen path's KV.)

## 2026-11-08: The server's chunk warm-up wrote into live expert records (flex tail) — BUG, fixed; the bb9e370 serve256k numbers withdrawn

With the chunk buffers as views into the arena's tail (bb9e370), the server's warm-up still ran `chunk_warm`'s dry
pass (every chunk kernel once, so no kernel is first loaded while an expert job is posted) without taking the tail:
the pass wrote the chunk buffers, which were the coldest records still in the VRAM table. Symptoms: generations
collapsed (the 8192 arm's tier counters showed almost every selection served from VRAM, i.e. the routing had
degenerated), and a serve256k "win" of 76.8 / 27.4 ms per token at 4096 that was 30% better than anything real.
qx-ds-check never calls `chunk_warm` outside `forward_chunk_opt`, which takes the tail first, so every tail check
and ds-checks passed. Fix: `chunk_warm` calls `flex_enter` itself (a no-op inside a chunk pass) and the worker calls
`chunk_release` after the warm-up; the release copies the evicted records straight back. Withdrawn: serve256k on
bb9e370 (8192 105.5; 4096 76.8). Standing: the 16k tails (chunk pass first), the TTFT timings (the prompt's chunk
pass evicted the tail before computing), every qx-ds-check bench. Lesson for the flex design: any writer of the
chunk buffers must hold the tail; a debug assert in `chunk_warm`/`forward_chunk_opt` that `flex_want` is set is the
cheap guard, added with the fix.

`tools/compare_dumps.py`, every generation of the no-chunk reference arm (8 prompts, temperature 0) is identical to

## 2026-11-08: Where the host's part of a layer goes (timeline counters) and what the pool does alone (`qx-poolbw` by core count) — MEASURED

Host-side timeline in the driver's stats line (`serve N calls: read / issue / total / gap`, chunk35; the counters
are cumulative over the run, so the 256k figures carry the cold first tokens): at 1k, 41,000 calls (1,024 tokens x
40 layers), reading the mailbox and scoring 1 us, issuing the staged copies 6 us, the whole call 558 us, the gap
between calls (the GPU's time between two jobs as the host sees it) 456 us; at 256k 865 / 1,289 us over the whole
run. So the mailbox protocol and the copy issue are nothing; the call is the pool.

The pool alone (`~/qx-bins/qx-poolbw`, synthetic IQ2_XXS/IQ2_S records of the DeepSeek geometry in huge pages, one
record per call unless noted): 1 core 2.55 ms per record (3.9 GB/s); 4 cores 0.667 (14.8 GB/s); 8 cores 0.368 (26.8);
16 cores 0.230 (42.9 GB/s), the same at batches 2, 6 and 64 (0.222-0.224). Scaling 11.1x on 16 cores: the two
barriers and the row split cost about 30% against a perfect split (0.16), and 43 GB/s is near this box's measured
DRAM ceiling (52-59), so the pool alone is close to memory-bound. Inside the engine the same records cost 0.37 ms
each (the 22 ms pool window at 256k less its 4.8 ms of NVMe waits, over ~47 records): 1.6x the bench. The gap is the
environment, not the kernel: candidates are the core clocks under two spinning threads (driver, engine) and memory
bus contention with the staged DMA (24 records of 9.5 MB per token out of pinned RAM) and the promotions; the
clock sample runs next (`CLOCKS`). What the dataflow-units redesign (family 15 item 9) could recover is the 30%
inside the pool, after the 1.6x.

## 2026-11-08: Core clocks during decode — MEASURED: the pool cores run at 5.3 GHz while generating; clocks are not the 1.6x

`/proc/cpuinfo` MHz sampled every 0.1 s over the generation phase of a forced 1k decode (1,024 tokens, 34.7 ms per
token): the 16 pool cores average 5,295 MHz (p10 5,268, p90 5,390), the SMT siblings 3,452; the pool bench alone
(`qx-poolbw`, 16 cores, pure compute) runs at 5,080 (p10 4,980). So the spinning driver and engine threads do not
cost the pool its clocks (spinning on PAUSE between jobs is lighter than compute, and the cores sit at the boost
ceiling), and Big-MoE-Small-Box's MWAITX result (their lane cores 4.34 -> 4.8 GHz) does not transfer to this box:
family 15 item 10 is closed. What is left for the 0.23 -> 0.37 ms per record gap between the bench and the engine:
memory-bus contention with the staged DMA out of pinned RAM (test: decode256k with the decode staging share at 0,
all RAM misses through the pool, no DMA), and the pinned tier's 4 KiB pages against the bench's huge pages (test:
`QX_POOLBW_HUGE=0`). Both queued (`CONTEND`).

## 2026-11-08: The pool's per-record time in the engine: DMA contention 7%, page size nothing — MEASURED; thread placement is next

decode256k on chunk35 with the decode staging share at 0 (every RAM miss through the pool, no staged DMA) against 3/8:
pool-served records 5,954 vs 3,931 per 64 tokens, pool window less NVMe waits 1,980 vs 1,403 ms, so **0.333 vs 0.357 ms
per record** (decode256k 46.6 vs 45.0: the bus is worth 1.6 ms per token, nothing more). `qx-poolbw` on 4 KiB pages
(`QX_POOLBW_HUGE=0`) 0.233 vs 0.230 on huge pages. With the clock result (pool cores at 5.3 GHz while generating), the
1.45x between the pool alone (0.23) and the pool in the engine without DMA (0.33) is none of bus, pages or clocks.
Left: thread placement. The pool pins its workers to cores 0-15 (the driver thread is worker 0 on core 0); the engine
thread and the other threads are unpinned and spin on CUDA syncs, so one of them can share a pool core, and the
pool's two barriers per call make every job wait for its slowest worker. Test: the pool on 0-13 with the process
confined to cores 14-15 (and their siblings) for everything else, against 0-13 unconfined and the default.

## 2026-11-08: The engine thread on its own physical core, the pool on the other 15 — LANDED (decode256k 45.0 -> 42.0, -6.9%); set D run queued

Thread placement during generation (`ps -L` while a forced 1k decode generates, AFFIN2): the 16 pool workers on
CPUs 0-15 at ~72% each, the engine thread (its CUDA sync spins) at 73% on CPU 17, the SMT sibling of pool core 1,
and a CUDA helper thread on CPU 16, the sibling of core 0. A pool worker sharing its physical core with a spinning
thread runs at a fraction of its speed, and the pool's two barriers per call make every job wait for that worker:
that is the 0.23 -> 0.33 ms per record between the pool alone and the pool in the engine (DMA 7%, pages and clocks
nothing). decode256k, chunk35, three repeats each: engine thread pinned to physical core 15 with the pool on 0-14
**42.00 (0.2%) and 42.00 (0.5%)**; engine thread pinned to CPU 31 (the sibling of pool core 15) 44.50 (0.4%); the
unpinned default 45.00 (3.3%) and 45.10. A dedicated core is the fix; a sibling is not. The pool on 14 cores with
the engine unpinned 46.20 (the pool window scales with cores). An earlier note (findings, 2026-11-03: "pinning the
engine thread changed nothing") was taken at 1k context when the pool was not the critical path.

Default now (chunk36): `pool_cpus()` = every physical core but the last (sysfs `thread_siblings_list`, SMT
siblings folded), `main_cpu()` = the last physical core, pinned at the start of the engine thread in qx-ds-check and
in the server's worker; `QX_DS_CPUS` and `QX_DS_MAIN_CPU` (`none` to leave the thread unpinned) override. The
confined-process variant (`taskset` on the whole process) deadlocked the engine (never posted a job) and is not the
way to do it. Exactness untouched (placement only); ds-checks and the full set D run on chunk36 follow.

## 2026-11-08: Set D on the placement default (chunk36 = df104c7) — 29.316 (was 30.047), correctness pass; awaiting the adjudicator; chunk37 supersedes it

`perf/results/2026-11-08/setd-pin-164650`, `--check` pass: decode256k 42.20 (1.7%; was 45.10), prefill256k 5.60 (0),
serve256k3 106.61 (0.8%; was 107.41), decode 33.80 (4.7%; was 34.60), prefill128 10.80 (0.9%), serve3 96.43 (0.3%).
Score 30.047 -> 29.316 (-2.4%), carried by decode256k (-6.4%). The breaker's review of df104c7
(`perf/ideation/2026-11-08-breaker-placement.md`): the pin ran before CUDA initialised, so CUDA's driver and event
threads and rayon's fill workers inherited the one-CPU mask (tier fill 8.3 -> 12.0 s, warm-up 15 -> 22 s in these
logs), the 45 -> 42 cannot be attributed between the engine thread and the CUDA helper that moved with it, pins
ignored failures (the taskset deadlock), `physical_cores()` stopped at the first hole, no pool cap. Fixed in 5d185f2
(chunk37): pin after the fill and before the driver, helpers on the engine core's siblings (`QX_DS_HELPERS=free` to
compare), topology from the online/allowed CPUs, checked pins, pool capped at 16. Chunk37's own arms (sibling vs
free helpers, fill times) and set D run (`setd-place`) follow; this row records the one-CPU variant.

## 2026-11-08: Why the placement gain needs the pin BEFORE CUDA initialises — MEASURED (chunk36 42.5/42.7 vs chunk40 44.2-44.7, same session)

The breaker's review of the first placement (pin at process start, before CUDA and the tier fill) led to chunk37-40:
pin after the fill, helpers moved to the engine core's sibling / the same CPU / left free, a CUDA scheduling knob.
None reproduced chunk36: decode256k, same session, alternating: chunk36 (early pin, `QX_DS_CPUS=0-14
QX_DS_MAIN_CPU=15`) 42.50 (1.4%) and 42.70 (1.2%); chunk40 default (late pin, helpers on the same CPU) 44.7/44.5 and
44.20 (4.1%); chunk37 helpers on the sibling 44.20 / 44.30; helpers free 44.50; `QX_CUDA_SCHED=yield` 44.0 / 47.8;
`=blocking` deadlocks the engine (never posts a job, like the taskset arm). The host counters of every late-pin arm
are ~12-25% slower (pool 1830 vs 1640 ms per 64 tokens, serve gap 1.5 vs 1.2 ms per layer).

A per-interval thread sampler (`/proc/<pid>/task/*/stat` deltas every 0.5 s) names the difference: with the early
pin, CUDA's worker thread `DSD-0-0` is busy for a few seconds around warm-up and idle during generation (7 busy
samples); with the late pin it is busy on the engine's CPU throughout generation (104 samples), time-sharing with
the engine thread's sync spins. The CUDA driver creates its worker under the mask it finds at context creation:
one CPU gives a quiet worker, 32 CPUs a spinning one, and moving it afterwards does not change its mode. rayon is
load-time only (the fill, planar repacking), so its workers are not the cause; the fill regression of the early pin
(8 -> 12 s) came from rayon sizing its pool under the one-CPU mask.

Fix (chunk41): `pin_engine_early()` at process/engine-thread start builds rayon's global pool on every physical
core first (threads keep the affinity they are created with), then pins the engine thread; CUDA then initialises
under the one-CPU mask; `place_threads` still tidies stragglers before the driver starts. Arms queued after the
identity and prediction runs: chunk41 default twice, then ds-checks and set D. Presupposition row 24 (the `gap`
counter) gains a fact: part of the gap was the CUDA worker's spin, not GPU time.

the RAM tier's place reuse. Queued (`IDENT2`): the same prompts on chunk41, chunk34 and chunk38; set D on chunk41
(`setd-pin2`) runs only if chunk41 is identical. Until then nothing from chunk33 on is promoted.

## 2026-11-08: The layer's routing is predictable before its attention runs — presupposition row 5 OVERTURNED (measurement, chunk38)

`QX_DS_PREDICT=pre` (ec924e7): at the top of each layer the layer's own router runs on the pre-attention residual
(the FFN pre-mix terms computed from the same residual, the FFN norm, the router GEMV, the top-12 with the bias),
into the predictor's own scratch and a mailbox region; the driver scores the top-6 / 8 / 12 against the layer's real
selections and against the host-served ones (pool + staged). decode256k (64 tokens from the 262k state): all
selections 12,043 / 13,013 / 13,711 of 15,400 = **78.2 / 84.5 / 89.0%**; host-served 4,523 / 4,972 / 5,274 of 5,972
= **75.7 / 83.3 / 88.3%**. decode (1,024 tokens at 1k): 78.9 / 85.3 / 89.8% and 73.9 / 82.2 / 88.1%. ds-checks pass
with the predictor on (it writes nothing the real pass reads). The previous-token signal, the only one measured
before (2026-11-07), was 13-23%; colibri's layer-ahead figure on GLM was 71.6%. So the belief that nothing can be
fetched before the router runs ("the next layer's routing is unknown, so no prefetch lever", family 2) was wrong
for this model: three quarters of the host-served experts are known about 0.5 ms (one attention + dense phase)
before the post, while the bus is idle.

What it buys: host-served records per layer ~1.5; predicted top-8 covers 83% of them; staging the predicted
non-resident records during the attention + dense phase costs ~0.24-0.4 ms of bus per layer (within the idle 0.48)
and turns them into VRAM hits at post time, so the pool is left with the unpredicted ~17-25%: the 16 ms host window
at 256k shrinks toward 4-6 ms, decode256k 42 -> ~30-33 ms. Design (exact by construction: the same records through
the same kernels, only earlier): the predictor bumps a prediction-sequence word; the driver, idle during that
phase, copies predicted RAM-resident misses into a per-layer ring of speculative staging slots and writes a
device-side `prestaged[g]` address table behind each copy (stream-ordered); `qx_moe_post` treats a miss with
`prestaged[g] != 0` as resident at that address (no host wait); slots recycle when the next layer's post arrives;
predicted NVMe records are read into RAM early through the promotion ring. Serve (width >= 2) stays as is: it is
bytes-bound and wrong predictions cost bytes. The `next` variant (one layer further ahead) is measured next: more
time for NVMe at some recall cost.

## 2026-11-08: `QX_DS_PREDICT=next` (the next layer's router on this layer's FFN input) — MEASURED: 58-75% of host-served selections

Same harness as the `pre` measurement (notebook 100), chunk38: decode256k all selections 10,258 / 11,345 / 12,370 of
15,399 = **66.6 / 73.7 / 80.3%** (top-6 / 8 / 12), host-served 3,484 / 3,973 / 4,461 of 5,976 = **58.3 / 66.5 / 74.7%**;
decode at 1k 67.0 / 73.9 / 80.5% and 54.0 / 62.9 / 72.0%. Against `pre` (78 / 85 / 89% and 76 / 83 / 88%): one expert
phase plus an attention further ahead costs 10-18 points of recall. decode256k with the predictor running 45.20
(the extra router GEMV and top-12 per layer are within the noise of the 45.0-45.1 default). Use: `pre` for the
speculative staging (0.5 ms is enough for RAM-resident records at 0.19 ms each); `next` is the candidate for the
NVMe-only predictions (a 9.5 MB read takes ~1.4 ms, which `pre` cannot hide), where even 60% recall on ~7 records
per token would cover most of the 4.8 ms of fetch waits. Not built yet: the prefetch (chunk42, `QX_DS_PREFETCH`)
queues NVMe-only `pre` predictions for RAM promotion only.

## 2026-11-08: Identity bisect — chunk34 and chunk43 identical, chunk38 and chunk40 differ on one late token under concurrency (open)

The 8 temperature-0 prompts at 262144 / 3 slots against the chunk32 no-chunk reference: chunk34 (server default

prompt at concurrency 3 diverges at character 872 of 992; chunk40 (late pin, helpers on the same CPU) 7/8: the same
prompt, diverging at 909; chunk43 (early pin before CUDA, rayon wide, topology cached) 8/8. The same prompt alone
(concurrency 1) is identical in every build. Two divergence positions on one prompt, only in the two builds whose
threads were moved after the load, and clean in the builds before and after, look like a timing-dependent flip
rather than an arithmetic change: the late pin moved the HTTP, scheduler and NVMe threads onto one CPU with the
engine thread, which changes request arrival and tier timing. By contract the bits must not depend on either, so
if the flip is real it is an exactness hole in the tiers or the batched path at width 3 (ds-checks compare widths
8 and 64 to single steps, never 3). Next: a stress run (identity x3 on chunk43, x2 on chunk40) to tell a per-build
effect from a random flake; if chunk43 stays clean and chunk40 fails again, diff what the late pin changes at run
time (thread placement of the NVMe and scheduler threads) and reproduce under qx-ds-check with a width-3 window
check added to ds-checks. Nothing from chunk33 on is promoted until the stress run is clean.

## 2026-11-09: All-row mixed windows expose cold-width compressor corruption — CANDIDATE fix

Resumed at 61679b2. The previous session's completed chains (`~/qx-chains/`, copied to
`perf/results/2026-11-09/identity-resume/`) report eight clean server comparisons: frozen tiers x3, common
staging share 3 x3, baseline x2. Rechecked with the stricter comparator: all identical. Narrow mixed windows
passed 128/128 and 512/512 with adapting tiers and 512/512 frozen. The adapting 512 run nevertheless logged
CUDA_ERROR_DEINITIALIZED after its success line: the checker's process::exit skipped driver cleanup.

Hypothesis: the checker misses prompt rows and wider scheduler windows. It previously compared only A at
row zero, at widths 2,3,5,7,4,6. Extended the tool with explicit widths, rotation preserving per-slot order,
per-slot single-step references (`QX_DS_MULTI_CHECK_ALL=1`), and startup warming as a control.

| Run | A exact | B exact | C exact | Result |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Broad widths, A only, rotate, N=128 | 128/128 | unchecked | unchecked | pass; no teardown panic after joining driver |
| Broad widths, all rows, rotate, N=26, cold widths | 26/26 | **1/834** | 26/26 | fail, first B position 1 at width 4 / row 0 |
| Same all-row schedule, startup-warmed, unfixed engine | 26/26 | **834/834** | 26/26 | pass |
| Same cold all-row schedule, pending buffers preserved | 26/26 | **834/834** | 26/26 | pass |

Broad widths = 2,3,4,6,8,12,16,24,32,48,64,96,128. Pack and golden profile unchanged, default requested
20 GiB VRAM / 60 GiB RAM, three slots; actual VRAM tier about 17 GiB. These are correctness runs, not comparable
performance measurements. Logs are `perf/results/2026-11-09/{multi-wide,multi-all,multi-all-warm,pending-warmup-fix}/`.
The fixed binaries are isolated in `~/qx-bins/resume-20261109/`; hashes are in the fix run's provenance.json.

Mechanism: at ratio 2, a dry pass through B positions 1 and 2 overwrites pending index 0 with dry position 2.
The real position 1 then consumes that value instead of position 0. Saving every pending buffer as bytes before
the dry pass and restoring after synchronization fixes this history dependency. The real pass rewrites current
ring cells and committed store rows before consuming them. Presupposition 26 is overturned. No CUDA source or
arithmetic changes; added cost is confined to first use of each width. The new minimal regression in ds-checks
uses cold widths 2,3,4 and three tokens per slot.

Tooling: compare_dumps now rejects extra occurrences/keys, empty dumps, missing text fields, and changes between
reasoning and text that concatenation hid. The benchmark's stale count of two successful window lines rejected
the now-four-line window suite; check_ds_log validates each named result exactly once, production numerics
included, and detects conflicting duplicate results. CPU regression tests pass; release build passes. Independent
breaker review found no defect in the final pending-buffer fix for prefetch disabled; review recorded in
`perf/ideation/2026-11-09-breaker-identity-tools.md`.

Decision: candidate pending full regression and server identity, no promotion or performance claim. The original
intermittent server difference is **not resolved**: that server warms every scheduler width before requests, and
its short identity prompts execute zero actual chunks. Next discriminating server pair: window 1 versus 128;
window 1 still permits three concurrent decoders. Full validation results follow below when complete.

Validation follow-up: the fixed cold-width all-row case is 886/886 exact; the full updated ds-checks suite passes
all nine named results (including 6/6 KL 0, 57/59 KL 0.128643, all four 128-row comparisons, tokenizer,
rewind/park, and the new three-slot cold-width check). Server identity on the same fixed binaries is **7/8**:
French Revolution at concurrency 3 diverges in reasoning at character 400, the known original symptom.
`perf/results/2026-11-09/pending-warmup-server/compare.log` records it. This preserves the promotion hold and
separates the verified cold-width fix from the unresolved server issue. Paired window-1 control follows.

Window-1 control completed on the same fixed binaries and server settings: **8/8 responses identical**,
including French Revolution; scheduler widths only 1,2,3, no chunk/KV-only steps. Logs:
`perf/results/2026-11-09/pending-warmup-server-window1/`. The default-window pair's one failure against this
clean control points toward wide prompt execution or its resulting state/placement, but is **INCONCLUSIVE**
with one control and an intermittent symptom. Do not change the server default or promote from this pair.
Next: repeat paired controls; a teacher-forced all-row test using this actual prompt and reference continuation;
then fixed/adapting tiers in that reproducer. Validation jobs have finished; changes remain uncommitted and
installed binaries and the promoted score (30.047) are unchanged.

## 2026-11-09: The late-token flip under three streams — ROOT CAUSE FOUND AND FIXED (a staged selection that fell back to the pool was computed on a stale x)

**Method.** `QX_DS_SCHED_TRACE` records every engine call of a run (resets, rewinds, windows, chunks) with an FNV hash
per returned logits row; `qx-ds-replay` re-executes such a file and compares hashes; `QX_DS_TRACE_LAYERS=1` adds a
hash of every row's residual after each layer and the driver's lane lists per layer. A server identity run that
failed (tr-2: hash-map prompt, concurrency 3, character 909) was recorded and replayed
(`perf/results/2026-11-09/sched-trace/`):
- the passing run's trace replays with 0 of 4,626 rows different (the replay is faithful);
- the failing run's trace gives two bit-identical families: {the server run, adaptive windowed replays a, b} and
  {single steps with adapting tiers, windows with frozen tiers, single steps with frozen tiers}, differing in the same
  76 rows from row 3497 (slot 2 at position 222); of six adaptive replays, four landed in the first family;
- layer traces: the first difference is layer 0 of forward 1228, row 0, where expert 33 is in BOTH the staged list and
  the pool list in the wrong family (pool `[(33, 5)] inram [false]`), and only staged in the reference.

**Cause.** `qx_moe_post` copied a token's x to the mailbox only when its own pool-miss count was non-zero ("x crosses
PCIe only when the CPU has experts to compute"). When a staged selection's record is evicted from RAM between the
post and the serve (a RAM promotion's victim), the driver falls back to the pool for it (`serve`: "evicted between
the post and now"). If that selection is the job's only pool work, the mailbox holds the x of an earlier job, and
the pool computes the right expert on the wrong input: one wrong expert output, deterministic given the tier
history (which is why runs agreed bit for bit), reachable only with adapting tiers, staging (width >= 2 stages
8/8, so the post's own pool list is often empty) and an eviction in that window. Single-sequence ds-checks and
`qx-ds-multi` never hit it; frozen tiers cannot.

**Fix (chunk48).** `kernels/step.cu`: x is copied whenever anything is host-served (pool misses + staged), and
`qx_moe_wait` waits in that case too (a job with no pool misses of its own was not waited for; the staged wait
happened to cover it in the default mode). `driver.rs`: the fallback's slot-word writes are synchronized before the
job is acknowledged (a real ordering hole, though not the cause: chunk47 with only this change still failed), and
fallbacks are counted (`stage fallbacks N` in the stats line). Validation: replays of the failing schedule with
adapting tiers 5 of 5 identical to the frozen reference on all 4,626 rows (before: 2 of 6); server identity x6 and
ds-checks running. Cost: 20 KB of x per token over PCIe on jobs with staged selections only; set D will say.

**Lessons.** (1) An intermittent, timing-dependent wrong answer was a deterministic function of the tier history:
record and replay first, statistics second (a morning of isolation arms said nothing). (2) Every fallback path
needs its own test: this one had none. (3) Belief overturned (presuppositions): "a job with no pool misses needs no
x on the host" was false as soon as the driver could move a selection between lanes.

## 2026-11-09: The "bimodal placement" is host memory pressure, not the placement — MEASURED; standard-settings runs are unreliable while the win10 VM is up

Placement arms on decode256k (3 repeats each): chunk49 `QX_DS_HELPERS=free` 44.3 / 46.6 / 44.2; chunk49 default
44.7 / 44.2 / 44.9; chunk36 (the plain early pin that was fast in ~15 repeats on 2026-11-08) 44.3 / 44.5 / 42.2.
Twenty minutes earlier the same chunk49 default gave 42.0 at 1.2% spread. So the mode is not a property of the
build. During a slow run: `kcompactd0` at 91% of a CPU (9 h of CPU time accumulated) on CPU 16, the SMT sibling of
pool core 0 (the driver thread's core); a qemu vCPU of the win10 VM at ~20% on CPU 4 (a pool core); `ksmd` 9%;
MemFree 0.9 GB, swap 8.0 of 8.0 GB used, memory pressure `some avg10` 47%, CPU pressure 38%. The engine's standard
decode tier (QX_DS_RAM=100: 107.5 GB resident) plus the VM's 16 GB allotment overcommit the 120 GB box: the kernel
compacts and reclaims continuously on the pool's cores (the slow mode: pool +11%, serve gap 1.2 -> 1.5 ms per
layer), and systemd-oomd kills runs at random (four today). The standard settings were chosen with the VM off
(goal memory, 2026-11-03).
Consequences: (1) the placement's gain (45.1 -> 42.0) is real whenever the box is not under pressure, and chunk43+
is as good as chunk36; the earlier "one fast run in three" reading was this noise. (2) No set D run at standard
settings can be gated or promoted while the VM runs; the 29.983 run mixes modes. (3) Relative A/B arms are run at
QX_DS_RAM=86 in both arms until the VM question is settled. Decision needed from the user: stop the VM for
benchmark runs, or lower the standard RAM tier and re-baseline (a standard-settings change, which is a stop
condition of the goal).

## 2026-11-09: Prefetch with the shared-mix predictor (`QX_DS_PREDICT=mix`), standard tier, clean box — CANDIDATE (-3.0%; budget closed)

Hypothesis: the `pre` predictor's 1.3 ms was mostly the hc terms it recomputed; the layer computes the same terms
(`hc_terms(hc_attn)` into `pre_a`) at the top of `layer_attn`, so a predictor launched right after them needs only
the mix application, one norm and the router GEMV. Counter: decode256k, and the predictor-only arm.
Change: `QX_DS_PREDICT=mix` (mode 3), chunk52. Kill criterion written before the build: below 3% against the
control at the standard tier, the prefetch stops here (build 6 of the 7 budgeted).

Reading: `mix` 40.6 against 41.85 (mean of the controls' medians): -3.0%; `pre` -2.3%. The predictor alone still
costs +1.1 ms (four launches per layer: mix application, norm, router GEMV, top-k), so the copies themselves are
worth 2.3 ms and a free predictor would give about -5.5%. The host side gets what the outsider's arithmetic
predicted (host call 707 -> 627 us per layer, 14 pool records and 8 staged per token removed), and the GPU side
gives 2.2 ms of it back (gap +56 us per layer: 0.9 the predictor, the rest the 24 more records per token that GPU
expert kernels now run and the post). Two copies per layer lose again (issued 4,318, used 2,160): the second copy
runs into the host window. ds-checks with `mix` K=6 one copy: pass (all nine lines).
Verdict: CANDIDATE, off by default. A complexity-adding change needs 8%; the measured ceiling of this design (one
copy per layer, the lead of one attention phase) is ~5.5% with a free predictor. Budget closed at six builds. Re-open
if a source of lead longer than one attention phase appears (a probe two layers ahead, or copies that do not
contend with the pool for DRAM: NVMe-to-BAR1 peer-to-peer, backlog).

## 2026-11-09: How much a GiB of VRAM tier is worth at 256k — MEASURED (presupposition row 8: payoff revised down)

decode256k, clean box, three repeats: 15.4 GiB tier (1,613 records; the standard) 41.8 x3; 13.0 GiB (1,354)
43.5 / 43.6 / 43.6; 11.0 GiB (1,145) 44.3 / 44.4 / 44.4. VRAM-served selections per token 147.1 / 139.4 / 131.5.
Slope: 0.75 ms per GiB over the last 2.4 GiB, 0.40 over the 2 before: flattening as the tier grows (the ranking's
tail). Extrapolated, the ~8 GiB that the latent KV rows hold would be worth at most 3.5-4.5 ms as experts
(likely less: the slope keeps falling), against +0.9 to +3.3 ms for gathering 512 rows per layer per token from
host memory: net -3 ms at the very best, around zero at the measured 7 GB/s gather rate. Row 8's predicted
"-7 ms of waits vs +3 of gathers" does not survive; the latent-rows-to-host build is not worth starting for
decode256k. (For serve256k3, which is bytes-bound, each GiB is worth more: re-rate there if serve stays the gap.)
